A time-like window into tensionless worldsheets
Sudip Karan, and Bibhas Ranjan Majhi

Abstract
Rindler worldsheets are known to acquire a Carrollian structure at infinite acceleration, marking their tensionless limit. This work extends the same paradigm to time-evolving worldsheets in the background target spacetime spanning the Kasner wedges. Specifically, we demonstrate that approaching the null horizons of the Kasner worldsheet induces a Carrollian structure, necessitating an infinite limit on the time-evolution parameter. We further examine how the associated Bogoliubov transformations on the usual tensile Kasner worldsheets – encompassing quantum modes, vacuum states, and oscillators – provide insights into their yet-unexplored tensionless regime. Intriguingly, phenomena such as null string complementarity (i.e., the emergence of open string physics from closed strings) and Hagedorn physics naturally arise in the quantum vacuum of tensionless worldsheets. These findings validate that time-like entanglement in Kasner worldsheets is not merely analogous to, but exactly equivalent to, space-like entanglement in Rindler worldsheets, viewed in distinct causally-disconnected regions.



1
Introduction

Carrollian structures and Carrollian physics have emerged as a compelling tool with a wide range of applications. For a comprehensive review of these developments, we direct readers to the reviews
deBoer:2023fnj
;
Hansen:2021fxi
. The so-called Carroll regime is fundamentally tied to two complementary approaches. First, it naturally arises in the ultra-relativistic limit, where the speed of light (
c
) approaches zero,
c\to 0

Levy-Leblond:1965
;
SenGupta:1966qer
.
1
1

1

Readers should note that if the system in question has a characteristic velocity
v_{c}
, the ultra-relativistic limit for the Carroll regime should be interpreted as
c/v_{c}\to 0
.
As a related aspect, Carroll gravity can be derived via a systematic ‘small
c
’ expansion of general relativity (GR), which can be viewed as a perturbative expansion around the Carroll point
c\to 0
.
2
2

2

An equivalent approach to the ‘small
c
’ expansion involves setting
c=\epsilon\hat{c}
, where
\epsilon
is a dimensionless parameter, and expanding around
\epsilon\to 0
.
Alternatively, Carrollian geometry emerges on null hypersurfaces, such as event horizons or conformal boundaries, due to the degenerate nature of the induced spacetime metric
Hartong:2015xda
. These insights have found extensive application in string theory, particularly in the study of tensionless limits at high energies
Bagchi:2013bga
;
Bagchi:2015nca
;
Bagchi:2020fpr
, string dynamics in black hole spacetimes
Bagchi:2023cfp
, and microstate modeling of black holes
Bagchi:2022iqb
.

To date, string theory remains arguably the most promising framework for constructing a quantum theory of gravity. It generalizes the concept of point particles (as employed in quantum field theory) to one-dimensional fundamental strings, thereby offering a natural basis for a quantum theory of GR. These strings are characterized by an intrinsic length scale
\ell_{s}
, which determines their tension through the relation
\alpha^{\prime}\sim\ell_{s}^{2}
(e.g., see (
8
)). In non-interacting regime, the tension of strings acts as the sole free parameter and leads to two extreme limits. The infinite tension limit (
\mathcal{T}\to\infty
) corresponds to the point-particle limit (
\ell_{s}\to 0
), where strings lose their distinctive ‘stringy’ nature (
\alpha^{\prime}\to 0
), and string theory reduces to the low-energy sector of GR and supergravity. Conversely, the tensionless limit (
\mathcal{T}\to 0
) propels strings into an ‘ultra-stringy’ regime (
\alpha^{\prime}\to\infty
), where they become infinitely stretchable (
\ell_{s}\to\infty
), pushing the framework into its ultra-high-energy sector, far from the end of GR and supergravity. Notably, the tensionless limit bears some similarities to the point-particle limit, akin to the massless limit in point-particles, where the string worldsheet becomes null
Schild:1976vq
and corresponds to the “null” sector of string theory. This underscores the significance of investigating the tensionless limit of string theory to gain a deeper understanding of how strings perceive generic null surfaces and, possibly the spacetime singularities.

This work focuses on exploring the broad direction of probing into the tensionless regime of closed-string worldsheets by enabling their Carrollian structure. Carrollian strings naturally arise on the worldsheet when their tension vanishes
Bagchi:2013bga
or when the background target spacetime possesses a Carrollian structure
Cardona:2016ytk
. Detailed studies of this
\text{tensionless}\leftrightarrow\text{Carrollian}
regime, both classical and quantum, have revealed intriguing aspects of string theory to date.
3
3

3

For details, readers are referred to the discussions in
Bagchi:2013bga
;
Bagchi:2015nca
;
Bagchi:2016yyf
;
Bagchi:2017cte
;
Bagchi:2019cay
;
Banerjee:2023ekd
;
Banerjee:2024fbi
;
Bagchi:2020fpr
;
Cardona:2016ytk
and the references therein.
A key insight is that the degrees of freedom underlying tensionless strings are fundamentally distinct from those in their usual tensile state. Interestingly, the Bogoliubov transformations (involving quantum modes, vacuum states, and oscillators) on tensile worldsheets at the Carrollian limit help probe into their tensionless regime.

It has long been speculated (e.g., see
Francia:2007qt
) that the distinction between closed and open strings becomes blurred as soon as the tensionless limit is approached. Bagchi et al. in
Bagchi:2015nca
;
Bagchi:2019cay
provided a concrete demonstration of how an open string description and related symmetries emerge from closed strings in the tensionless limit, which is contrary to the conventional wisdom. They discovered the existence of a Bose-Einstein condensation of all perturbative closed string degrees of freedom onto the emergent open string, further speculating a new phase transition at the point of vanishing string tension
Bagchi:2019cay
. Furthermore, a picture of “null string complementarity” has been observed for accelerated Rindler worldsheets approaching their tensionless limit
Bagchi:2020ats
;
Bagchi:2021ban
. Interestingly, all these manifestations of the closed-to-open string transition are deeply connected to strings near the Hagedorn temperature where a novel phase transition introduces new degrees of freedom
Atick:1988si
. At this extreme high-temperature limit of string theory, strings effectively become tensionless
Pisarski:1982cn
;
Olesen:1985ej
, favoring the formation of long strings over heating up a gas of strings
Bowick:1989us
;
Giddings:1989xe
. Building on these insights, we aim to extend the Carrollian analysis of inertial (Minkowski) and accelerated (Rindler) worldsheets to the tensionless dynamics of a time-evolving, i.e., Kasner worldsheet.

Let us now briefly elucidate and motivate the structure of the tensionless Kasner worldsheet in comparison to its Rindler counterpart. The Rindler worldsheet is analogous to an accelerated observer moving within a background target spacetime spanning space-like separated Rindler wedges (e.g., R and L in
fig.

1
). It has been established that, in the limit of infinite acceleration, the Rindler worldsheet manifests to a tensionless state
Bagchi:2020ats
;
Bagchi:2021ban
. This transition effectively corresponds to reaching the accelerated worldsheet horizon by inducing a degenerate metric structure. Consequently, this links the Carroll limit of the background target spacetime to a Carroll limit of the accelerated worldsheet. In the present work, our objectives are threefold. First, we extend the analysis by replacing the target spacetime with one that spans time-like separated future (expanding) and past (shrinking) wedges (e.g., F and P in
fig.

1
). This worldsheet setup defines a structure analogous to a time-evolving observer, which we recognize as the
Kasner worldsheet
. Next, we embed the standard Kasner physics of quantum field theory
Socolovsky:2013rga
;
Olson:2010jy
;
Higuchi:2017gcd
;
Quach:2021vzo
into the string worldsheet theory. Specifically, we consider quantum modes living in the two causally disconnected Kasner worldsheet wedges. These two sets of modes are inherently independent, yet their entanglement must suffice to describe the entire Kasner worldsheet via a quantum mode expansion. Notably, this ‘time-like entanglement’ has been shown to have an interpretation exactly analogous to the conventional entanglement between modes in the Rindler worldsheet wedges
Olson:2010jy
(also see
Quach:2021vzo
;
Barman:2024dql
). Finally, we hypothesize that the mode functions of the Kasner worldsheet must relate to their inertial (i.e., Minkowski) counterparts via Bogoliubov transformations, analogous to the Rindler case, but parameterized by the dynamics of time evolution. Our ultimate goal is to determine whether limiting the time-evolution parameter – and correspondingly the mode functions and Bogoliubov coefficients – can enable the construction of a Carrollian structure for the Kasner worldsheet. Such a Carrollian structure would expected to manifest as the ‘tensionless limit’ of time-evolving string worldsheets.

In the Kasner sector, the only possible trajectories are those along which the Kasner time is evolving, while the space-like coordinate remains constant. Along these trajectories, the Minkowski spacetime expands in the F region. In fact, for a specific choice of coordinates that covers a part of the Milne universe, this has quite a resemblance with the Kasner universe
book:Birrell
;
mukhanov2005physical
. So, we expect that the physics of Kasner worldsheet can be important to explore the cosmological aspects of strings. On the other hand, the black hole interior and white hole region in Kruskal coordinates carry similarities with the Kasner sectors. So, the physics of Kasner sectors also bears importance in exploring black hole interiors as well as white hole regions. These connections provide additional importance of concentrating on the Kasner worldsheet.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In
section

2
, we provide a concise review of the foundational aspects of ordinary Kasner physics, a textbook overview of the worldsheet theory of closed tensile strings, and the setup and limits of tensionless string worldsheets. In
section

3
, we introduce the novel framework of time-evolving Kasner worldsheets, formulating their quantum mode expansion, quantum vacuum state, and associated Bogoliubov transformations. Subsequently,
section

4
explores the possible limits on a closed Kasner worldsheet, leading to the emergence of Carrollian structures and the corresponding tensionless physics. Finally,
section

5
summarizes the key findings and discusses the broader implications of the tensionless Kasner worldsheets. Given the technical intricacies of the subject, we include
appendix

A
to present detailed formulations of the ‘global modes’ essential for structuring the Kasner worldsheet expansion discussed in
section

3
.

2
Preliminaries: a quick tour to the basics

2.1
The Kasner physics

The inertial metric describing a Minkowski spacetime in global coordinates is given by

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{\rm M}=c^{2}dt^{2}-dx^{2}-d\bm{x}_{\perp}^{2},

(1)

where
\bm{x}_{\perp}
represents coordinates that are transverse to the plane
(ct,x)
. The future Kasner wedge F
(ct>\lvert x\rvert)
and past Kasner wedge P
(ct<\lvert x\rvert)
as depicted in
fig.

1
can be connected to the Minkowski spacetime (
1
) via adopting the following transformations (e.g., see
Socolovsky:2013rga
;
Olson:2010jy
;
Higuchi:2017gcd
;
Quach:2021vzo
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\rm(F)~:~}t=a^{-1}e^{a\xi}\cosh\left({a\eta/c}\right),\quad x={ca^{-1}}e^{a\xi}\sinh\left({a\eta/c}\right),\\[6.0pt]
&{\rm(P)~:~}t=-a^{-1}e^{a\xi}\cosh\left({a\eta/c}\right),\quad x=-{ca^{-1}}e^{a\xi}\sinh\left({a\eta/c}\right).\end{split}

(2)

Figure 1:
Trajectories of the Minkowski observers in future and past Kasner wedges F
(ct>\lvert x\rvert)
and P
(ct<\lvert x\rvert)
with coordinates
(\xi,\eta)\in(-\infty,+\infty)
and bounded by the null horizons
\mathcal{J}^{+}(\xi=-\infty,x=+ct,\eta=+\infty)
and
\mathcal{J}^{-}(\xi=-\infty,x=-ct,\eta=-\infty)
. The space-like regions R
(x>c\lvert t\rvert)
and L
(x<c\lvert t\rvert)
are analogous right and left Rindler wedges.

Any specific Minkowski observer, satisfying the transformations (
2
) and their inverse (
5
), moves along a fixed constant
\eta
coordinate line, intersecting with all the constant
\xi
hyperbolas in the Kasner wedges F and P, as described by:

\displaystyle c^{2}t^{2}-x^{2}={c^{4}\kappa^{-2}},\qquad{ct}=x\,{\coth}\left({a\eta/c}\right),

(3)

where
a
is introduced as a real parameter with units of
\rm sec^{-1}
, following the convention in
Olson:2010jy
;
Quach:2021vzo
. Throughout this paper, we refer to
a
as the
Kasner parameter
. Notably,
\kappa=ac\,e^{-a\xi}
represents an evolution in time analogous to the proper acceleration for Rindler observers. The coordinates
\xi
and
\eta
are adopted respectively as the ‘time’ and ‘space’ coordinates of the Kasner spacetime (in both the F and P wedges), resulting in the following metric

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{\rm K}=e^{2a\xi}\left(c^{2}d\xi^{2}-d\eta^{2}\right)-d\bm{x}_{\perp}^{2}.

(4)

The inverse of the transformations (
2
) is given by,

\displaystyle\eta={c\over 2a}\ln\left({ct+x\over ct-x}\right),\quad\xi={1\over 2a}\ln\left({a^{2}\over c^{2}}\left(c^{2}t^{2}-x^{2}\right)\right).

(5)

Therefore, at any moment, one can interpret
ac
as the redefined time-evolution parameter (analogous to the redefined acceleration for Rindler observers) of an observer in Kasner spacetime (
4
) (expanding in F and contracting in P). The Kasner- or time-evolution becomes infinite when one hits the null horizons at
\xi\to-\infty
and
\eta\to\pm\infty
. Notably, for a Rindler observer with a uniform acceleration
a^{\prime}
in the R and L wedges (see
fig.

1
), the scaling parameter
a
should be substituted by
a^{\prime}/c
.

Note that here the
\eta=
constant straight lines are timelike, and therefore, any motion of an object must follow these trajectories. Use of (
3
) yields,

t=\frac{e^{a\xi}}{a}\cosh\left(a\eta/c\right)~.

(6)

So these trajectories can be described by the above relation, where on the right-hand side, only
\xi
is changing along the path of a moving object. Moreover, in the F sector, as the object moves along these trajectories, the space increases. Therefore the trajectories can be related to the expansion of space. The P sector corresponds to the reverse one, i.e., contraction of space. In fact, this Kasner expanding sector has a close resemblance with the Milne universe. It is specifically an open universe with nonzero spatial curvature and vanishing spacetime curvature and represents an expanding Universe.
For a specific choice of coordinates that covers a part of the Milne Universe, the metric is identical to that for the Kasner region
book:Birrell
;
mukhanov2005physical
. On the other hand the black hole interior geometry in Kruskal coordinates bears considerable similarity with Kasner, except the presence of singularity. Therefore, understanding physics in the Kasner universe must illuminate both the cosmology and black hole sides.

2.2
Worldsheet theory of tensile closed strings

We start by revisiting a closed tensile bosonic string moving in a
D
-dimensional Minkowski background spacetime with metric
\eta_{\mu\nu}
, (
\mu,\nu=0,\ldots,D-1
). The related Polyakov action, which describes
D
massless scalar fields
X^{\mu}(\sigma^{a})
coupled to the two-dimensional dynamical worldsheet metric
\gamma_{ab}(\sigma^{a})
with coordinates
\sigma^{a}=(\tau,\sigma)
for
a,b=0,1
, is given by
Polchinski:1998rq

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathcal{S}_{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{S}_{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{S}_{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{S}_{{{\rm P}}}}=-{\frac{\mathcal{T}}{2}}\int d\tau d\sigma\sqrt{-\gamma}\,\gamma^{ab}\partial_{a}X^{\mu}\partial_{b}X^{\nu}\eta_{\mu\nu},

(7)

where
\gamma=\det\gamma_{ab}
and
\mathcal{T}
is the string tension related to the conventional Regge slope parameter
\alpha^{\prime}
(in units of string length squared) as

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}={1\over 2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}.

(8)

In the conformal gauge, where
\gamma_{ab}=\text{diag}(-1,1)
, and under the periodic boundary condition for the closed string (along the spatial coordinate
\sigma
with arbitrary periodicity
\ell
),

\displaystyle X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma+\ell),

(9)

the most general solution to the equations of motion corresponding to (
7
) is usually expressed in a standard mode expansion, as described in
Polchinski:1998rq
. However, the objective of this work requires restructuring the conventional mode expansion of the worldsheet solution and expressing it in the following form, which resembles a simple Klein-Gordon massless scalar field solution:

\displaystyle X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=x^{\mu}+\alpha^{\prime}p^{\mu}\tau+\sqrt{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{n>0}\left[\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{n}+\alpha_{n}^{\mu}\mathcal{V}_{n}+\tilde{\alpha}_{-n}^{\mu}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{n}^{*}+\alpha_{-n}^{\mu}\mathcal{V}_{n}^{*}\right],

(10)

where
\tilde{\alpha}_{0}^{\mu}={\alpha}_{0}^{\mu}=\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}\over 2}p^{\mu}
. This solution describes the motion of the string’s center-of-mass, with position
x^{\mu}
and momentum
p^{\mu}
, while carrying a superposition of an infinite collection of right- and left-moving oscillation modes, with coefficients
\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu}
and
{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu}
, respectively. The right-moving
\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{n}
and left-moving
\mathcal{V}_{n}
modes, along with their Hermitian conjugates
\{\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{n}^{*},\mathcal{V}_{n}^{*}\}
, are successively expressed as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{n}&={ie^{-i\omega_{n}(\tau-\sigma/c)}\over\sqrt{4\pi}n},\qquad\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{n}^{*}=-{ie^{i\omega_{n}(\tau-\sigma/c)}\over\sqrt{4\pi}n},\\[10.0pt]
\mathcal{V}_{n}&={ie^{-i\omega_{n}(\tau+\sigma/c)}\over\sqrt{4\pi}n},\qquad\mathcal{V}_{n}^{*}=-{ie^{i\omega_{n}(\tau+\sigma/c)}\over\sqrt{4\pi}n}.\end{split}

(11)

It is important to note that the parameter
\omega_{n}={2\pi cn\over\ell}
serves as the frequency for the above mode functions on a closed inertial worldsheet with arbitrary periodicity
\ell
, satisfying the closed string condition (
9
). The oscillators
\left\{\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu},{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu}\right\}
are essentially a rescaled creation operators (for
n<0
) and annihilation operators (for
n>0
), satisfying the commutation relation
4
4

4

For each mode
n
and oscillation direction
\mu
, the commutation (
12
) satisfy a quantum harmonic oscillator algebra with the standard normalization
\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu}\to\sqrt{n}\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu}
,
\tilde{\alpha}_{-n}^{\mu}\to\sqrt{n}(\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu})^{\dagger}
, and similarly for the holomorphic counterparts.

\displaystyle\left[\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu},\tilde{\alpha}_{m}^{\nu}\right]=\left[{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu},{\alpha}_{m}^{\nu}\right]=n\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta_{n+m,0},

(12)

and related via their respective hermitian conjugates
\tilde{\alpha}_{-n}^{\mu}=(\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu})^{\dagger}
and
{\alpha}_{-n}^{\mu}=({\alpha}_{n}^{\mu})^{\dagger}
. Furthermore, they annihilate a Minkowski worldsheet vacuum state
\lvert 0_{\rm M}\rangle
, defined to obey:

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu}\lvert 0_{\rm M}\rangle={\alpha}_{n}^{\mu}\lvert 0_{\rm M}\rangle=0,\kern 5.0pt\forall~n>0.

(13)

2.3
Tensionless strings and their worldsheet theory

There are two distinct but consistent approaches for formulating tensionless string theory from its tensile counterpart. The first approach, as outlined in
Isberg:1993av
, involves constructing the action and formulating the tensionless theory from first principles, providing an
intrinsic look at tensionless strings
. The second approach, known as the limiting approach
Bagchi:2013bga
;
Bagchi:2015nca
, involves taking the appropriate limit on worldsheet coordinates derived from the tensile closed string theory. This limiting process, referred to as
worldsheet contraction
, results in the so-called “ultra-relativistic limit” or “Carrollian limit”
Duval:2014lpa
, where the worldsheet speed of light tends to zero. Our goal is to briefly revisit both approaches to access their mutual integrity, which will essentially lead us to re-derive the Bogoliubov transformations between the tensionless operators and their tensile counterparts within the context of the inertial Minkowski worldsheet of closed strings.

Revisiting the theory of a tensile worldsheet propagating in a Minkowski target space, one can observe that the action (
7
) becomes ill-defined when tension
\mathcal{T}
of fundamental strings vanishes. However, as demonstrated in
Isberg:1993av
, there is a systemic treatment to construct an intrinsic action describing the tensionless string worldsheets,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{0}=\int d\tau d\sigma\,V^{a}V^{b}h_{ab},\quad h_{ab}=\partial_{a}X^{\mu}\partial_{b}X^{\nu}\eta_{\mu\nu},

(14)

where
h_{ab}
acts as a induced metric which is degenerate or “null” (i.e.,
\det h_{ab}=0
) on the tensionless worldsheet.
V^{a}
is introduced as a vector density replacing the tensile metric density
\mathcal{T}\sqrt{-\det\gamma_{ab}}\,\gamma^{ab}\to V^{a}V^{b}
at
\mathcal{T}=0
. In the transverse gauge,
V^{a}=(1,0)
, and by incorporating the closed string periodicity condition (
9
), the field equations describing the tensionless worldsheet (
14
) were originally solved through a mode expansion involving intrinsic yet anharmonic oscillators
Bagchi:2015nca
. However, it is possible to transform the entire setup into the framework of the standard harmonic oscillator basis, expressing the mode expansion for the tensionless closed Minkowski worldsheet as

\displaystyle X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=x^{\mu}+\hat{\alpha}^{\prime}\hat{p}^{\mu}\tau+\sqrt{2\pi\hat{\alpha}^{\prime}}\sum_{n>0}\left[\tilde{c}_{n}^{\mu}\tilde{V}_{n}+c_{n}^{\mu}V_{n}+c_{-n}^{\mu}\tilde{V}_{n}^{*}+c_{-n}^{\mu}V_{n}^{*}\right],

(15)

where
\tilde{c}_{0}^{\mu}={c}_{0}^{\mu}=\sqrt{\hat{\alpha}^{\prime}\over 2}\hat{p}^{\mu}
represents the zero-mode part in terms of the string’s center-of-mass momentum
\hat{p}^{\mu}
, and the constant
\hat{\alpha}^{\prime}
(with dimensions of length-squared) replaces the analogous
{\alpha}^{\prime}
parameter in the tensile mode expansion (
10
). The harmonic oscillators
\{\tilde{c}_{n}^{\mu},c_{n}^{\mu},\tilde{c}_{-n}^{\mu},c_{-n}^{\mu}\}
correspond to the operators of right- and left-moving tensionless worldsheet modes. These operators do not interact with each other and satisfy the commutation relations,

\displaystyle\big[\tilde{c}_{n}^{\mu},\tilde{c}_{m}^{\nu}\big]=\big[{c}_{n}^{\mu},{c}_{m}^{\nu}\big]=n\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta_{n+m,0},

(16)

with their respective Hermitian conjugates
\tilde{c}_{-n}^{\mu}=(\tilde{c}_{n}^{\mu})^{\dagger}
and
{c}_{-n}^{\mu}=({c}_{n}^{\mu})^{\dagger}
. The right- and left-moving
\{\tilde{V}_{n},V_{n}\}
, along with their conjugates
\{\tilde{V}_{n}^{*},V_{n}^{*}\}
have a frequency
\omega_{n}={2\pi cn\over\ell}
, are successively given by:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{V}_{n}&={i\left(1-i\omega_{n}\tau\right)e^{i\omega_{n}\sigma/c}\over\sqrt{4\pi}n},\qquad\tilde{V}_{n}^{*}=-{i\left(1+i\omega_{n}\tau\right)e^{-i\omega_{n}\sigma/c}\over\sqrt{4\pi}n},\\[10.0pt]
V_{n}&={i\left(1-i\omega_{n}\tau\right)e^{-i\omega_{n}\sigma/c}\over\sqrt{4\pi}n},\qquad V_{n}^{*}=-{i\left(1+i\omega_{n}\tau\right)e^{i\omega_{n}\sigma/c}\over\sqrt{4\pi}n}.\end{split}

(17)

Next, we will explore how the
c
-oscillators intrinsically characterize the quantum structure of a tensionless worldsheet and how they map to their tensile counterparts, i.e., the
\alpha
-oscillators. To do this, we must revisit the framework developed in
Bagchi:2013bga
;
Bagchi:2015nca
, which introduces a parametric space by limiting the tensile theory, corresponding to the string tension being exactly zero. This approach successfully recovers all the physics and results associated with intrinsic tensionless strings. The basic premise of this limiting approach lies in the physical interpretation of the tensionless limit of fundamental strings. As the tension approaches zero, fundamental strings become long and floppy, and their length tends to infinity. Technically, this situation can be realized in worldsheet coordinates by setting the limit
\sigma\to\infty
. However, directly applying
\sigma\to\infty
would disrupt the “closedness” condition (
9
) of strings, since their ends are fixed and identified in terms of a periodic
\sigma
. Instead of taking
\sigma\to\infty
, we can alternatively consider a limit where
Bagchi:2015nca
,

\displaystyle\sigma\to\sigma,\quad\tau\to\epsilon\tau,\quad\alpha^{\prime}\equiv{\hat{\alpha}^{\prime}\over\epsilon},\quad\epsilon\to 0.

(18)

The above approach is referred to as the
worldsheet contraction
. The tensionless limit (
18
) corresponds to an infinite boost limit
\bm{\beta}={\bm{\sigma}\over c\tau}\to\infty
on the worldsheet. Consequently, this infinite boost tensionless limit can also be understood as the so-called
Carrollian
or
ultra-relativistic
limit
Duval:2014uoa
;
deBoer:2021jej
,
5
5

5

Since the Minkowski worldsheet is inertial, the infinite boost limit
\bm{{\beta}}={\bm{v}\over c}\to\infty
can be achieved only by taking the Carrollian limit
c\to 0
for a uniform worldsheet velocity
\bm{v}
.

\displaystyle c\to\epsilon c,\quad\bm{{\beta}}\equiv{\bm{\hat{\beta}}\over\epsilon},\quad\epsilon\to 0,

(19)

where
\bm{\hat{\beta}}
is the Carroll boost parameter. This algebraic realization sets the speed of light
c
to zero on the closed string worldsheet, rendering it a null or degenerate manifold. Notably, one must adopt either limit (
18
) or (
19
) exclusively to avoid ambiguity in structuring a tensionless worldsheet. By carefully implementing the typical limit (
18
) at every stage of the tensile worldsheet setup in
section

2.2
, one can validate the quantum mode expansion (
15
) of an intrinsic tensionless string worldsheet. This process leads to deriving the intrinsic tensionless oscillation operators in terms of the tensile
\alpha
operators. Finally, switching to the harmonic
c
-oscillator basis precisely reproduces the mode expansion (
15
) associated with the contracted quantum modes (
17
), resulting in the following Bogoliubov transformations between the tensionless and tensile mode creation and annihilation operators (for all
n>0
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathchoice{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}&=\beta_{+}\mathchoice{\tilde{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{\tilde{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{\tilde{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{\tilde{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}+\beta_{-}\mathchoice{{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}},\qquad\mathchoice{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}=\beta_{+}\mathchoice{{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}+\beta_{-}\mathchoice{\tilde{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{\tilde{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{\tilde{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{\tilde{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}},\\[6.0pt]
\mathchoice{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}&=\beta_{+}\mathchoice{\tilde{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{\tilde{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{\tilde{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{\tilde{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}+\beta_{-}\mathchoice{{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}},\qquad\mathchoice{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}=\beta_{+}\mathchoice{{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}+\beta_{-}\mathchoice{\tilde{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{\tilde{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{\tilde{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{\tilde{\alpha}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}},\end{split}

(20)

where
\beta_{\pm}={1\over 2}\left(\sqrt{\epsilon}\pm{1\over\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)
. One must keep in mind that all the above identifications remain valid as
\epsilon\to 0
. However, the current framework of the tensionless worldsheet induces a parametric evolution in
\epsilon
that defines a natural ‘flow’ valid throughout the parameter space
\epsilon\in[1,0]
. This flow essentially leads us to interpret the
c
-oscillators (
20
) as a set of evolving creation and annihilation operators
\{\mathchoice{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}(\epsilon),\mathchoice{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}(\epsilon),\mathchoice{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}(\epsilon),\mathchoice{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}(\epsilon)\}
, which interpolate between the original tensile pairs
\left\{\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu},{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu},\tilde{\alpha}_{-n}^{\mu},{\alpha}_{-n}^{\mu}\right\}
at
\epsilon=1
and their tensionless counterparts
\{\mathchoice{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}},\mathchoice{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}},\mathchoice{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}},\mathchoice{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{-n}}}}\}
as
\epsilon\to 0
. These operators indeed annihilate an evolving vacua
\lvert 0_{c}(\epsilon)\rangle
, which changes with respect to the
\epsilon
parameter:

\displaystyle\lvert 0_{c}(\epsilon)\rangle:~\mathchoice{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{\tilde{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}(\epsilon)\lvert 0_{c}(\epsilon)\rangle=\mathchoice{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}{{c}^{{{\mu}}}_{{{n}}}}(\epsilon)\lvert 0_{c}(\epsilon)\rangle=0,\kern 5.0pt\forall~n>0.

(21)

The tensionless vacuum state
\lvert 0_{c}\rangle
is obtained as a special limiting case of the tensile evolving vacua (
21
), i.e.,
\lvert 0_{c}\rangle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\lvert 0_{c}(\epsilon)\rangle
.

3
Kasner road to time-evolving worldsheets

This section elucidate the setup of embedding the Kasner physics on string target space and worldsheets, followed by exploring their quantum mode expansion and the Bogoliubov transformations of related oscillators.

3.1
From Minkowski to Kasner worldsheet: the embedding setup

Now, we aim to explore the closed string worldsheet moving in a Kasner target spacetime, which resides in the analogous F and P regions as in
fig.

1
. In this context, we first express the target spacetime metric for the Minkowski worldsheet (
7
) as

\displaystyle ds^{2}=\left(cdX^{0}\right)^{2}-\left(dX^{1}\right)^{2}-\left(dX^{i}\right)^{2},\quad\forall~i\in(2,D-2).

(22)

We then consider the inertial components
\{X^{0},X^{1}\}
subject to the standard Kasner transformations (
2
) into a new accelerated set
\{\bar{X}^{0},\bar{X}^{1}\}
while keeping the other
D-2
spatial directions
X^{i}
invariant, yielding:

\displaystyle X^{0}=\pm a^{-1}e^{a\bar{X}^{0}}\cosh\left({a\bar{X}^{1}/c}\right),\kern 5.0ptX^{1}=\pm{ca^{-1}}e^{a\bar{X}^{0}}\sinh\left({a\bar{X}^{1}/c}\right),\kern 5.0ptX^{i}=\bar{X}^{i},

(23)

where
\pm
denotes the F and P wedges, respectively. The above transformations, in turn, embed the closed string worldsheet into a time-evolving (expanding in F and contracting in P) Kasner target spacetime having a proper Rindler acceleration analogous
ac
. Then the metric describing this new target spacetime is given by,

\displaystyle ds^{2}=e^{2a\bar{X}^{0}}\left[\left(cd\bar{X}^{0}\right)^{2}-\left(d\bar{X}^{1}\right)^{2}\right]-\left(dX^{i}\right)^{2},

(24)

where the related Kasner horizons correspond to the regime
\bar{X}^{0}\to-\infty
and
\bar{X}^{1}\to\pm\infty
by setting an infinite time-evolution limit
{a\over c}\to\infty
. Notably, throughout this work, we always consider the closed string with a Kasner worldsheet effectively propagating only in two directions, and the
i
-directions do not depend on the related worldsheet coordinates.

It is also important to note the notion of two distinct observers associated with the Minkowski and Kasner worldsheets: one being an inertial observer traveling through flat Minkowski spacetime, and the other is an time-accelerated observer moving within Kasner spacetime. The induced metric on the newly embedded worldsheet is expressed as follows

\displaystyle h_{ab}\propto e^{2a\bar{X}^{0}}\partial_{a}X^{\mu}\partial_{b}X^{\nu}\eta_{\mu\nu},\quad\forall~\mu,\nu\in(0,1).

(25)

This metric remains regular everywhere except in the limit where
\bar{X}^{0}\to-\infty
, which corresponds to approaching the horizons of the Kasner target space. In this limit, the metric (
25
) degenerates as its conformal factor
e^{2a\bar{X}^{0}}
becomes non-invertible and vanishes. In other words, when embedding a worldsheet into a Kasner target space, certain ‘divergent points’ also emerge on the worldsheet metric. These points can be interpreted as the locations where a worldsheet horizon is induced and coincides with the target space horizon. In
section

4
, we will also explore how these worldsheet divergent points correspond to the limit
{a\over c}\to\infty
where the Kasner worldsheet becomes ‘null’ or ‘tensionless’.

Arguably, the worldsheet of a string must uphold the same physical structure as the target spacetime in which it is embedded
Bagchi:2021ban
. More precisely, when a string propagates in a Kasner background, the new target space induces a similar Kasner structure on the accelerated worldsheet of the string. Therefore, when considering the mapping
X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)\to\bar{X}^{\mu}(f(\tau,\sigma))
, we must associate the Kasner worldsheet with new coordinates
(\xi,\eta)
, which are reparametrizations of their Minkowski counterparts
(\tau,\sigma)
, given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\rm(F)~:~}\tau=a^{-1}e^{a\xi}\cosh\left({a\eta/c}\right),\quad\sigma={ca^{-1}}e^{a\xi}\sinh\left({a\eta/c}\right),\\[10.0pt]
&{\rm(P)~:~}\tau=-a^{-1}e^{a\xi}\cosh\left({a\eta/c}\right),\quad\sigma=-{ca^{-1}}e^{a\xi}\sinh\left({a\eta/c}\right).\end{split}

(26)

These transformations induce a two-dimensional metric structure for the closed string Kasner worldsheet,

\displaystyle ds^{2}=e^{2a\xi}\left(c^{2}d\xi^{2}-d\eta^{2}\right).

(27)

Here all the related trajectories describing the evolution of the Kasner worldsheet are divided into the causally disconnected regions F
(c\tau>\lvert\sigma\rvert)
and P
(c\tau<\lvert\sigma\rvert)
. These regions are bounded by two horizons, defined in terms of the worldsheet coordinates as

\displaystyle c\tau=\pm\sigma,\kern 5.0pt\eta=\pm\infty,\kern 5.0pt\xi=-\infty,

(28)

where the worldsheet time-evolution hits the limit
a\to\infty
. At this point, we can also define the inverse mapping between the coordinates of the Minkowski and Kasner worldsheets via

\displaystyle\eta={c\over 2a}\ln\left({c\tau+\sigma\over c\tau-\sigma}\right),\quad\xi={1\over 2a}\ln\left({a^{2}\over c^{2}}\left(c^{2}\tau^{2}-\sigma^{2}\right)\right).

(29)

Notably, this mapping diverges at the two singular points
c\tau=\pm\sigma
where the Kasner worldsheet metric (
27
) degenerates. This corresponds to the analogous notion of reaching the horizons of the associated Kasner target space, which in turn induces similar horizons on its worldsheet.

3.2
Periodicity and its regularization

However, it is important to note that the closed string worldsheet with Kasner or accelerated coordinates
(\xi,\eta)
is technically distinct from the worldsheet with Minkowski or inertial reparametrization
(\tau,\sigma)
. Arguably, the mapping from an inertial to an accelerated worldsheet actually induces “folds” on the associated closed strings
Bagchi:2021ban
. In this context, the divergent points
c\tau=\pm\sigma
associated with the mapping (
29
) are precisely where these folds appear. The incorporation of time-evolution on the worldsheet causes the closed string to become increasingly elongated, eventually becoming infinitely stretched at the divergent or fold points (e.g., see
fig.

3
). The entire scenario can be viewed as if the Kasner worldsheet splits into two causally disconnected pieces in the
(\xi,\eta)
parametrization, corresponding to
c\tau>\sigma
(future Kasner wedge F) and
c\tau<\sigma
(past Kasner wedge P). In other words, the Kasner worldsheet can be interpreted as two open strings glued together at the fold points
c\tau=\pm\sigma
, with appropriate boundary conditions to preserve the closed string properties.

In the above discussion, we now need to explore how the periodicity of the worldsheet is affected by the Kasner parametrization
(\xi,\eta)
. This is crucial in defining the ‘closedness’ condition for the string with Kasner worldsheet
\bar{X}^{\mu}(\xi,\eta)
. From the perspective of the Minkowski target space, the closed string worldsheet always satisfies the boundary condition,

\displaystyle X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=X^{\mu}(\tau+\Delta\tau,\sigma+\Delta\sigma),\kern 5.0pt\Delta\tau=0,\kern 5.0pt\Delta\sigma=\ell,

(30)

where
\ell
is an arbitrary periodicity of the Minkowski worldsheet explicitly associated with the coordinate
\sigma
. Evidently, when transitioning from the Minkowski to Kasner mapping
X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)\to\bar{X}^{\mu}(\xi,\eta)
, this condition (
30
) must be modified. For the closed string to remain closed in this new embedding, we must impose the following condition for
\mu=(0,1)
:

\displaystyle X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}\pm a^{-1}e^{a\bar{X}^{0}\left(\xi(\tau,\sigma),\eta(\tau,\sigma)\right)}\cosh\left({a\bar{X}^{1}\left(\xi(\tau,\sigma),\eta(\tau,\sigma)\right)/c}\right)\\
\\
\pm{ca^{-1}}e^{a\bar{X}^{0}\left(\xi(\tau,\sigma),\eta(\tau,\sigma)\right)}\sinh\left({a\bar{X}^{1}\left(\xi(\tau,\sigma),\eta(\tau,\sigma)\right)/c}\right)\end{array}\right.,

(34)

\displaystyle=X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma+\ell)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}\pm a^{-1}e^{a\bar{X}^{0}\left(\xi(\tau,\sigma+\ell),\eta(\tau,\sigma+\ell)\right)}\cosh\left({a\bar{X}^{1}\left(\xi(\tau,\sigma+\ell),\eta(\tau,\sigma+\ell)\right)/c}\right)\\
\\
\pm{ca^{-1}}e^{a\bar{X}^{0}\left(\xi(\tau,\sigma+\ell),\eta(\tau,\sigma+\ell)\right)}\sinh\left({a\bar{X}^{1}\left(\xi(\tau,\sigma+\ell),\eta(\tau,\sigma+\ell)\right)/c}\right)\end{array}\right..

With this periodic condition in the embedding of the non-inertial target space, the related Kasner worldsheet with
(\xi,\eta)
parametrization must be induced with an effective periodicity
\mathcal{L}_{\rm eff}
along the angular direction
\xi
,

\displaystyle\bar{X}^{\mu}(\xi,\eta)=\bar{X}^{\mu}(\xi+\Delta\xi,\eta+\Delta\eta),\kern 5.0pt\Delta\xi=\mathcal{L}_{\rm eff},\kern 5.0pt\Delta\eta=0.

(38)

Interestingly, this effective periodicity appears along the time-like coordinate
\xi
, which was space-like in the original setup (
30
) of its inertial (Minkowski) counterpart. To understand this, let us revisit the transformation (
3.2
), which shifts the worldsheet target space from Minkowski to Kasner. This transformation introduces a non-inertial frame evolving in time (measured by the Kasner parameter
a
) along
X^{0}
. Consequently, the Kasner worldsheet structure undergoes deformation due to stretching along the time-like
\xi
coordinate. To counterbalance this deformation, the closed Kasner worldsheet must incorporate the periodicity or boundary condition (
38
). It is also crucial to note that the equivalence between the ‘closedness’ conditions in
eqs.

30
and
38
is well-defined and comparable only at
\tau=0
.
6
6

6

This can be understood by considering the Kasner worldsheet as a hyperboloid with constant
\eta
slices forming elliptical cross-sections. In the inertial case, at constant
\tau
, smoothly connected circular slices appear parallel to the
x
-axis. The origins of the inertial
\tau
and non-inertial
\eta
slices coincide only at
\tau=0
.
However, this setting will induce a singularity (an artifact of the divergences due to the worldsheet horizon situated at
c\tau=\pm\sigma
) and hit our present setup for computing the effective periodicity
\mathcal{L}_{\rm eff}
. To regulate this, we consider that the closedness condition (
38
) must be accompanied by an effective periodicity in the following form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\rm eff}=\xi(0,\ell)-\xi(0,0)=\lim_{\varphi\to 0}{1\over 2a}\left[\ln\left({a^{2}\over c^{2}}\left(0-(\varphi+\ell)^{2}\right)\right)-\ln\left({a^{2}\over c^{2}}\left(0-\varphi^{2}\right)\right)\right].

(39)

In this simplistic setup, we set a constant parameter
\varphi
as the initial value of the inertial
\sigma
coordinate and define a regularized effective periodicity
\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}
for the Kasner worldsheet of closed strings, given as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}={1\over a}\ln\left(1+{\ell\over\varphi}\right)=\xi(0,\varphi+\ell)-\xi(0,\varphi).

(40)

Further, the above regularization setup needs to be appropriately accounted for in the original mapping (
26
) between Minkowski and Kasner worldsheets. The regularized transformations of their coordinates are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\rm(F)~:~}\tau=a^{-1}e^{a(\xi-\psi)}\cosh\left({a\eta/c}\right),\quad\sigma+\varphi={ca^{-1}}e^{a(\xi-\psi)}\sinh\left({a\eta/c}\right),\\[6.0pt]
&{\rm(P)~:~}\tau=-a^{-1}e^{a(\xi-\psi)}\cosh\left({a\eta/c}\right),\quad\sigma+\varphi=-{ca^{-1}}e^{a(\xi-\psi)}\sinh\left({a\eta/c}\right),\end{split}

(41)

where
\psi
is introduced as a regularization parameter in the Kasner worldsheet coordinates to capture the effect of considering the initial value
\xi(0,\varphi)
in step (
40
). Now, if we look at the two coordinate patches
(\tau,\sigma)
and
(\xi,\eta)
in the regularized mapping (
41
), there exists an offset between their origins. However, this mismatch can be adjusted by tuning the non-inertial parameter
\psi
in terms of the inertial parameter
\varphi
via,

\displaystyle\varphi=\pm{c\over a}e^{-a\left(\psi-{i\pi\over 2a}\right)}.

(42)

It is evident that as we approach the horizon
c\tau=\pm\sigma
of the non-inertial worldsheet, its time-evolution increases (i.e.,
{a\over c}\to\infty
), causing the regularization parameter
\varphi\to 0
while assuming
\psi
is positive.
7
7

7

To ensure that
\psi
remains positive in the definition (
42
), it is necessary to satisfy the condition
0<\lvert{a\varphi\over c}\rvert<1
. In the present analysis, we assume this inequality holds consistently, even as we approach the Kasner worldsheet horizon limit
{a\over c}\to\infty
, by carefully fixing the regularization parameter
\varphi\to 0
.
In this extremal limit, the effective periodicity approaches:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\rm eff}\to\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}\approx{1\over a}\ln\left({a\ell\over c}\right)+\psi.

(43)

At this point, the inverse transformations between the regularized worldsheet coordinates are expressed as

\displaystyle\eta={c\over 2a}\ln\left({c\tau+\sigma+\varphi\over c\tau-\sigma-\varphi}\right),\quad\xi-\psi={1\over 2a}\ln\left({a^{2}\over c^{2}}\left(c^{2}\tau^{2}-(\sigma^{2}+\varphi)^{2}\right)\right).

(44)

The above-developed setup of regularized coordinates and periodicity of the Kasner worldsheet will play a crucial role in the analysis of their quantum mode expansion in the next section.

3.3
Quantum mode expansions

We have already established that the two-dimensional metric structures of the Minkowski and Kasner worldsheets are conformally connected. Therefore, the Kasner worldsheet
\bar{X}^{\mu}(\xi,\eta)
must satisfy the same two-dimensional massless Klein-Gordon equations of motion (EOM) as the conformal gauge-fixed Minkowski worldsheet
X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)
:

\displaystyle\ \Box_{\xi,\eta}\bar{X}^{\mu}=0=\Box_{\tau,\sigma}X^{\mu},

(45)

along with the Virasoro constraints:

\displaystyle\partial_{\xi}\bar{X}^{\mu}\partial_{\eta}\bar{X}_{\mu}=0,\quad(\partial_{\xi}\bar{X}^{\mu})^{2}+(\partial_{\eta}\bar{X}^{\mu})^{2}=0,

(46)

where
\Box_{\xi,\eta}=(c^{-2}\partial_{\xi}^{2}-\partial_{\eta}^{2})
is the D’Alembertian on the worldsheet with
(\xi,\eta)
reparametrization. Consequently, we can now express the corresponding mode expansion solution, following the schematic form of (
10
) for the inertial worldsheet. Here, each mode-expansion component must be expanded with the associated mode functions and oscillation operators in both the F and P wedges for the Kasner worldsheet. In this context, the most general solution to the EOM (
45
) is given by:

\displaystyle\bar{X}^{\mu}(\xi,\eta)

\displaystyle=\bar{x}^{\mu}+\alpha^{\prime}\bar{p}^{\mu}\xi+\sqrt{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n>0;\\
\Lambda=\rm F,P\end{subarray}}\left[\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm\Lambda}}}}\tilde{\beta}^{{\kern-9.06946pt{\rm\Lambda}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm\Lambda}}}}\tilde{\beta}^{{\kern-9.06946pt{\rm\Lambda}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm\Lambda}}}}\tilde{\beta}^{{\kern-8.09724pt{\rm\Lambda}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm\Lambda}}}}\tilde{\beta}^{{\kern-8.09724pt{\rm\Lambda}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm\Lambda}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.06946pt{\rm\Lambda}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm\Lambda}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.06946pt{\rm\Lambda}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm\Lambda}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-8.09724pt{\rm\Lambda}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm\Lambda}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-8.09724pt{\rm\Lambda}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}+\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm\Lambda}}}}{\beta}^{{\kern-8.0483pt{\rm\Lambda}\kern 4.53441pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-4.14552pt\kern 4.53441pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm\Lambda}}}}{\beta}^{{\kern-5.31323pt{\rm\Lambda}\kern 2.77156pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-2.38268pt\kern 2.77156pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm\Lambda}}}}{\beta}^{{\kern-4.52136pt{\rm\Lambda}\kern 1.97969pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-1.5908pt\kern 1.97969pt{n}}}}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm\Lambda}}}}\mathcal{U}^{{\kern-9.4167pt{\rm\Lambda}\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern \displaystyle=\bar{x}^{\mu}+\alpha^{\prime}\bar{p}^{\mu}\xi+\sqrt{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{n>0}\left[\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\beta}^{{\kern-8.86531pt{\rm F}\kern . These oscillators satisfy the commutation relations,

\displaystyle\big[\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm\Lambda}}}}\tilde{\beta}^{{\kern-9.06946pt{\rm\Lambda}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern (48)

and are related via their respective Hermitian conjugates
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm\Lambda}}}}\tilde{\beta}^{{\kern-9.06946pt{\rm\Lambda}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{-n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm\Lambda}}}}\tilde{\beta}^{{\kern-9.06946pt{\rm\Lambda}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{-n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm\Lambda}}}}\tilde{\beta}^{{\kern-8.09724pt{\rm\Lambda}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{-n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm\Lambda}}}}\tilde{\beta}^{{\kern-8.09724pt{\rm\Lambda}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{-n}}}}=\big(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm\Lambda}}}}\tilde{\beta}^{{\kern-9.06946pt{\rm\Lambda}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm\Lambda}}}}\tilde{\beta}^{{\kern-9.06946pt{\rm\Lambda}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm\Lambda}}}}\tilde{\beta}^{{\kern-8.09724pt{\rm\Lambda}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm\Lambda}}}}\tilde{\beta}^{{\kern-8.09724pt{\rm\Lambda}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}\big)^{\dagger}

. Furthermore, they annihilate the Kasner worldsheet vacuum state
\lvert 0_{\rm K}\rangle
, defined to obey:

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\beta}^{{\kern-8.86531pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm (49)

We now turn to a critical aspect of this analysis: the form of the mode expansion functions that comprise the quantized Kasner worldsheet solution (
3.3
). These functions are assumed to have a structure similar to their Minkowski counterparts in (
11
). Within the context of the regularized
(\xi,\eta)
mapping depicted in
eqs.

41
and
44
, Kasner mode functions must be associated with a redefined and regulated frequency,

\displaystyle\Omega_{n}={2\pi cn\over\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}},

(50)

where
\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}
is the regularized periodicity (
40
) along the
\xi
-coordinate of the Kasner worldsheet. We now introduce the light-cone coordinates valid throughout the Minkowski worldsheet:

\displaystyle\sigma^{+}=c\tau+\sigma,\quad\sigma^{-}=c\tau-\sigma,

(51)

and their analogs in the Kasner worldsheet F and P wedges are:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\rm(F)~:~}\eta^{+}_{\rm F}=c\xi+\eta,\quad\eta^{-}_{\rm F}=c\xi-\eta,\\[6.0pt]
&{\rm(P)~:~}\eta^{+}_{\rm P}=-c\xi+\eta,\quad\eta^{-}_{\rm P}=-c\xi-\eta.\end{split}

(52)

Within our regularization scheme, these inertial and non-inertial sets are related as follows

\displaystyle\sigma^{+}+\varphi=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}+{c\over a}e^{{a\over c}\left(\eta^{+}_{\rm F}-c\psi\right)}&:\ \rm F\\
\\
-{c\over a}e^{-{a\over c}\left(\eta^{-}_{\rm P}+c\psi\right)}&:\ \rm P\end{array}\right.,~~~~~~\sigma^{-}-\varphi=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}+{c\over a}e^{{a\over c}\left(\eta^{-}_{\rm F}-c\psi\right)}&:\ \rm F\\
\\
-{c\over a}e^{-{a\over c}\left(\eta^{+}_{\rm P}+c\psi\right)}&:\ \rm P\end{array}\right..

In terms of the above light-cone coordinates, the right-moving (along
\eta^{-}_{\rm F}
and
\eta^{-}_{\rm P}
) and left-moving (along
\eta^{+}_{\rm F}
and
\eta^{+}_{\rm P}
) mode functions of frequency
\Omega_{n}
, as well as their Hermitian conjugates in the F and P wedges associated with the quantized Kasner worldsheet, can be reexpressed as follows:

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-8.86531pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-8.86531pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-7.95142pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-7.95142pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}

\displaystyle={ie^{-i\Omega_{n}\eta^{-}_{\rm F}/c}\over\sqrt{4\pi}n},\qquad\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-8.86531pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt{*}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-8.86531pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt{*}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-7.95142pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt{*}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-7.95142pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt{*}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}=-{ie^{i\Omega_{n}\eta^{-}_{\rm F}/c}\over\sqrt{4\pi}n},

(60a)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.0014pt{\rm P}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.0014pt{\rm P}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-8.04863pt{\rm P}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-8.04863pt{\rm P}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}

\displaystyle={ie^{-i\Omega_{n}\eta^{-}_{\rm P}/c}\over\sqrt{4\pi}n},\qquad\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.0014pt{\rm P}\kern 5.55557pt{*}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.0014pt{\rm P}\kern 5.55557pt{*}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-8.04863pt{\rm P}\kern 5.55557pt{*}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-8.04863pt{\rm P}\kern 5.55557pt{*}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}=-{ie^{i\Omega_{n}\eta^{-}_{\rm P}/c}\over\sqrt{4\pi}n},

(60b)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.21255pt{\rm F}\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.21255pt{\rm F}\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-6.07085pt{\rm F}\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-5.02084pt{\rm F}\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt{n}}}}

\displaystyle={ie^{-i\Omega_{n}\eta^{+}_{\rm F}/c}\over\sqrt{4\pi}n},\qquad\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.21255pt{\rm F}\kern 5.90282pt{*}}}_{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.21255pt{\rm F}\kern 5.90282pt{*}}}_{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-6.07085pt{\rm F}\kern 3.675pt{*}}}_{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-5.02084pt{\rm F}\kern 2.625pt{*}}}_{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt{n}}}}=-{ie^{i\Omega_{n}\eta^{+}_{\rm F}/c}\over\sqrt{4\pi}n},

(60c)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.34865pt{\rm P}\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.34865pt{\rm P}\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-6.16806pt{\rm P}\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-5.11806pt{\rm P}\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt{n}}}}

\displaystyle={ie^{-i\Omega_{n}\eta^{+}_{\rm P}/c}\over\sqrt{4\pi}n},\qquad\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.34865pt{\rm P}\kern 5.90282pt{*}}}_{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.34865pt{\rm P}\kern 5.90282pt{*}}}_{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-6.16806pt{\rm P}\kern 3.675pt{*}}}_{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-5.11806pt{\rm P}\kern 2.625pt{*}}}_{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt{n}}}}=-{ie^{i\Omega_{n}\eta^{+}_{\rm P}/c}\over\sqrt{4\pi}n}.

(60d)

3.4
Bogoliubov transformations

To better explore the Kasner worldsheet from the perspective of the Minkowski worldsheet, we aim to derive the relevant Bogoliubov transformations that connect their quantum modes and oscillation operators. For this purpose, we follow the standard and elegant method developed by Unruh
Unruh:1976db
. The process begins by considering the modes of the Kasner worldsheet that are analytically valid throughout the entire spacetime, including both the F and P wedges. However, it is important to note that the original (or local) Kasner modes (
60
) are non-analytic, as
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-8.86531pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-8.86531pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-7.95142pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-7.95142pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}=\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.21255pt{\rm F}\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.21255pt{\rm F}\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-6.07085pt{\rm F}\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-5.02084pt{\rm F}\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt{n}}}}=0
in P and
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.0014pt{\rm P}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.0014pt{\rm P}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-8.04863pt{\rm P}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-8.04863pt{\rm P}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}=\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.34865pt{\rm P}\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.34865pt{\rm P}\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-6.16806pt{\rm P}\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-5.11806pt{\rm P}\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt{n}}}}=0
in F. To resolve this, we need to restructure these local mode functions into appropriate linear combinations that allow for analytic continuation between the F and P wedges. In this analysis, we introduce these new combinations of modes as the “global modes” for the Kasner worldsheet at the Minkowski level. These are the so-called Unruh-Minkowski modes
Unruh:1976db
, which serve as the natural positive frequency Minkowski modes for translating between inertial and constantly accelerating frames.

The detailed derivation of the global modes for the Kasner worldsheet is not our primary focus here. Nevertheless, we have outlined the essential steps in
appendix

A
for readers interested in the intricacies of the process. The typical expressions presented in
eqs.

114
,
115
,
117
and
118
are unnormalized. To properly normalize these right- and left-moving global Kasner modes in the F and P wedges, we determine the appropriate normalization constants to make them orthonormal. Without delving into intermediate technicalities, we present the final normalized forms of the global Kasner modes as follows

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{{\kern-8.86531pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{{\kern-8.86531pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{{\kern-7.95142pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{{\kern-7.95142pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}

\displaystyle={1\over\sqrt{2\sinh\left(\pi\Omega_{n}\over a\right)}}\left(e^{\pi\Omega_{n}\over 2a}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-8.86531pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-8.86531pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-7.95142pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-7.95142pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}-e^{-{\pi\Omega_{n}\over 2a}}\,\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.34865pt{\rm P}\kern 5.90282pt{*}}}_{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-9.34865pt{\rm P}\kern 5.90282pt{*}}}_{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-6.16806pt{\rm P}\kern 3.675pt{*}}}_{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{U}}^{{\kern-5.11806pt{\rm P}\kern 2.625pt{*}}}_{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt{n}}}}\right),

The related inner products in the F and P regions, which we have defined in the following manner, are expressed as

(66)

Here, the global mode oscillators
\big\{\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\gamma}^{{\kern-8.86531pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\gamma}^{{\kern-8.86531pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\gamma}^{{\kern-7.95142pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\gamma}^{{\kern-7.95142pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}},\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}\tilde{\gamma}^{{\kern-9.0014pt{\rm P}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}\tilde{\gamma}^{{\kern-9.0014pt{\rm P}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}\tilde{\gamma}^{{\kern-8.04863pt{\rm P}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}\tilde{\gamma}^{{\kern-8.04863pt{\rm P}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}\big\}
and
\big\{\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}{\gamma}^{{\kern-7.51025pt{\rm F}\kern 4.20052pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-3.81163pt\kern 4.20052pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}{\gamma}^{{\kern-7.51025pt{\rm F}\kern 4.20052pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-3.81163pt\kern 4.20052pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}{\gamma}^{{\kern-4.93271pt{\rm F}\kern 2.53687pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-2.14798pt\kern 2.53687pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}{\gamma}^{{\kern-4.2079pt{\rm F}\kern 1.81206pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-1.42317pt\kern 1.81206pt{n}}}},\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\gamma}^{{\kern-7.64635pt{\rm P}\kern 4.20052pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-3.81163pt\kern 4.20052pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\gamma}^{{\kern-7.64635pt{\rm P}\kern 4.20052pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-3.81163pt\kern 4.20052pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\gamma}^{{\kern-5.02992pt{\rm P}\kern 2.53687pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-2.14798pt\kern 2.53687pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\gamma}^{{\kern-4.30511pt{\rm P}\kern 1.81206pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-1.42317pt\kern 1.81206pt{n}}}}\big\}
are similar to the creation operators (for

) and annihilation operators (for

\big\{\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{{\kern-8.86531pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{{\kern-8.86531pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{{\kern-7.95142pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{{\kern-7.95142pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}},\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{{\kern-9.0014pt{\rm P}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{{\kern-9.0014pt{\rm P}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{{\kern-8.04863pt{\rm P}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{{\kern-8.04863pt{\rm P}\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}\big\}
and
\big\{\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}{\mathcal{W}}^{{\kern-11.36534pt{\rm F}\kern 8.0556pt}}_{{\kern-7.66672pt\kern 8.0556pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}{\mathcal{W}}^{{\kern-11.36534pt{\rm F}\kern 8.0556pt}}_{{\kern-7.66672pt\kern 8.0556pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}{\mathcal{W}}^{{\kern-7.43196pt{\rm F}\kern 5.03612pt}}_{{\kern-4.64723pt\kern 5.03612pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}{\mathcal{W}}^{{\kern-5.99307pt{\rm F}\kern 3.59723pt}}_{{\kern-3.20834pt\kern 3.59723pt{n}}}},\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{W}}^{{\kern-11.50143pt{\rm P}\kern 8.0556pt}}_{{\kern-7.66672pt\kern 8.0556pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{W}}^{{\kern-11.50143pt{\rm P}\kern 8.0556pt}}_{{\kern-7.66672pt\kern 8.0556pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{W}}^{{\kern-7.52917pt{\rm P}\kern 5.03612pt}}_{{\kern-4.64723pt\kern 5.03612pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm P}}}}{\mathcal{W}}^{{\kern-6.09029pt{\rm P}\kern 3.59723pt}}_{{\kern-3.20834pt\kern 3.59723pt{n}}}}\big\}
. Since the global Kasner worldsheet modes satisfy the same analyticity property as the original Minkowski modes (

), they must share a common worldsheet vacuum state
\lvert 0_{\rm M}\rangle
, defined to obey:

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm F}}}}\tilde{\gamma}^{{\kern-8.86531pt{\rm F}\kern 5.55557pt{\mu}}}_{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt{n}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\rm (67)

As a result, the global mode Kasner operators
\gamma
are generally expressed as linear combinations of the Minkowski worldsheet operators
\alpha
(and vice versa), defining the same vacuum state as described in (
13
). Notably, the global mode operators are related to their local counterparts
\beta
through the following inner products in the expansion (
3.3
):

To evaluate the above inner products, we first expand the Kasner worldsheet
\bar{X}^{\mu}(\xi,\eta)
using equation (
3.4
). Subsequently, the associated global modes are decomposed into local modes via the definitions (
61
), with the orthonormality identities (
62
) and (

) applied. This procedure leads to the following relationships between the non-inertial and inertial annihilation operators:
